<a id='question-020'></a>

### Question 20: Cystic Fibrosis Transmembrane Conductance Regulator (CFTR) Gene Mutation & Nonsense-Mediated mRNA Decay (NMD)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the mechanism of Nonsense-Mediated mRNA Decay (NMD) as a cellular surveillance pathway that degrades mRNA transcripts containing premature termination codons (PTCs), specifically focusing on the requirement for the PTC to be located upstream of the last exon junction complex (EJC) for efficient NMD activation. (理解无义介导的 mRNA 降解 (NMD) 作为一种细胞监视途径，该途径降解含有提前终止密码子 (PTC) 的 mRNA 转录本，特别是关注 PTC 位于最后一个剪接复合体 (EJC) 上游才能有效激活 NMD 的要求。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old female presents to a genetics clinic for pre-conception counseling. She has a known history of cystic fibrosis (CF), diagnosed in childhood, characterized by chronic respiratory infections, pancreatic insufficiency, and elevated sweat chloride levels. Her partner is a carrier for a common CF mutation (ΔF508). Genetic testing reveals the patient carries a novel heterozygous mutation in the *CFTR* gene, designated c.4567G>A, p.(Gly1523*). This mutation introduces a premature stop codon (PTC) at codon 1523 within exon 10. Standard CFTR protein analysis shows significantly reduced levels of functional CFTR protein in her epithelial cells compared to controls. mRNA analysis of the *CFTR* transcript from her peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs) reveals a full-length transcript and a shorter, truncated transcript. The ratio of the full-length transcript to the truncated transcript is 2:1. The patient's physician is considering a potential therapeutic strategy targeting this specific mutation. Which of the following mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the reduced levels of the full-length *CFTR* mRNA transcript in this patient's cells?

(A) Activation of the unfolded protein response (UPR) leading to ER-associated degradation (ERAD) of the truncated CFTR protein, which secondarily inhibits transcription of the full-length mRNA.
(B) The premature stop codon (PTC) in the *CFTR* mRNA is located downstream of the last exon junction complex (EJC), preventing recognition by the NMD machinery.
(C) The truncated CFTR protein, despite containing the PTC, is efficiently translated and triggers proteasomal degradation via the ubiquitin-proteasome system (UPS), leading to a compensatory decrease in full-length mRNA transcription.
(D) The premature stop codon (PTC) in the *CFTR* mRNA is located upstream of the last exon junction complex (EJC), leading to the recruitment of NMD factors and subsequent degradation of the mRNA transcript.
(E) The mutation causes aberrant splicing, leading to the exclusion of exon 10, resulting in a non-functional CFTR protein and increased transcription of the full-length mRNA as a compensatory mechanism.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient History:** 32-year-old female with CF, known genetic condition.
*   **Mutation:** Novel heterozygous mutation c.4567G>A, p.(Gly1523*). This introduces a premature stop codon (PTC) at amino acid 1523 within exon 10.
*   **Protein Analysis:** Reduced functional CFTR protein levels.
*   **mRNA Analysis:** Presence of both full-length and truncated *CFTR* transcripts in a 2:1 ratio (full-length:truncated). This indicates that some full-length mRNA is produced, but it is being degraded or its production is reduced relative to the truncated mRNA.
*   **Question:** Asks for the mechanism responsible for the reduced levels of the *full-length* mRNA.

The key clue is the presence of a PTC within an exon (exon 10) and the observation of reduced full-length mRNA levels despite the presence of some full-length transcript. This points towards a cellular surveillance mechanism that targets transcripts with PTCs. The location of the PTC relative to the EJC is crucial for NMD activation.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **Nonsense-Mediated mRNA Decay (NMD):** NMD is a eukaryotic mRNA surveillance pathway that detects and degrades transcripts containing premature termination codons (PTCs). This prevents the production of potentially harmful truncated proteins.
*   **Mechanism of NMD:** In mammals, NMD is primarily triggered when a PTC is located more than 50-55 nucleotides upstream of the last exon junction complex (EJC) deposited on the mRNA. The EJC is a multiprotein complex that marks the boundary between exons.
*   **NMD Factors:** Key players include UPF1, UPF2, UPF3, and eRF1. When a ribosome encounters a PTC located upstream of the last EJC, eRF1 binds to the PTC, stalling the ribosome. This stalling allows UPF1 (bound to eRF1 and the PTC) to interact with UPF2, which is bound to the last EJC. This interaction recruits UPF3, forming a complex that activates a kinase cascade (e.g., involving ATM/ATR/Chk2). The activated kinase phosphorylates UPF1, leading to its dissociation from the ribosome and recruitment of mRNA decay factors (e.g., SMG1, CCR4-NOT complex), resulting in mRNA degradation.
*   **Application to the Case:** The patient has a PTC at codon 1523 within exon 10. Exon 10 is not the last exon of the *CFTR* gene (the *CFTR* gene has 10 exons). Therefore, the PTC is located upstream of the last EJC. This configuration meets the criteria for NMD activation. The NMD machinery recognizes the aberrant transcript, leading to its degradation. The observation of reduced full-length mRNA levels (compared to what would be expected if only the truncated protein was the issue) and the presence of a truncated transcript confirms that NMD is active and degrading the full-length mRNA. The ratio of 2:1 (full-length:truncated) indicates that NMD is partially effective; some full-length mRNA escapes degradation, leading to the production of some functional protein, but overall levels are reduced.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Activation of the unfolded protein response (UPR) leading to ER-associated degradation (ERAD) of the truncated CFTR protein, which secondarily inhibits transcription of the full-length mRNA.** While truncated proteins can trigger ERAD and UPR, the primary mechanism for reducing the *mRNA* level in the presence of a PTC is NMD, not secondary transcriptional inhibition due to protein degradation. ERAD targets misfolded proteins in the ER, not necessarily truncated proteins resulting from PTCs, and UPR primarily affects protein folding capacity, not directly mRNA levels in this context. The question specifically asks about the reduced *mRNA* levels.
*   **(B) The premature stop codon (PTC) in the *CFTR* mRNA is located downstream of the last exon junction complex (EJC), preventing recognition by the NMD machinery.** This is incorrect. NMD is typically activated when the PTC is *upstream* of the last EJC. If the PTC were downstream of the last EJC (i.e., within the last exon or 3' UTR), the EJC would shield the PTC from the NMD machinery, preventing its recognition and degradation. Since the mutation is in exon 10 (not the last exon), the PTC is upstream of the last EJC.
*   **(C) The truncated CFTR protein, despite containing the PTC, is efficiently translated and triggers proteasomal degradation via the ubiquitin-proteasome system (UPS), leading to a compensatory decrease in full-length mRNA transcription.** While truncated proteins are often degraded by the UPS, this mechanism primarily affects the protein level, not the mRNA level directly. Furthermore, there is no established mechanism for UPS-mediated degradation of a truncated protein to cause a *compensatory decrease* in the transcription of the full-length mRNA. NMD is the direct mechanism targeting the mRNA itself.
*   **(E) The mutation causes aberrant splicing, leading to the exclusion of exon 10, resulting in a non-functional CFTR protein and increased transcription of the full-length mRNA as a compensatory mechanism.** The vignette states the mutation is c.4567G>A, p.(Gly1523*), which introduces a PTC *within* exon 10, not causing its exclusion. Aberrant splicing leading to exon skipping is a different type of mutation. Also, NMD leads to mRNA degradation, not increased transcription of the full-length mRNA.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Nonsense-mediated mRNA decay (NMD) is a crucial cellular quality control mechanism that degrades mRNAs containing premature termination codons (PTCs). For NMD to be activated in mammals, the PTC must generally be located upstream of the last exon junction complex (EJC). This mechanism prevents the production of potentially harmful truncated proteins. Understanding the location of the PTC relative to the EJC is key to predicting whether NMD will occur. Differential diagnoses for reduced protein levels due to genetic mutations include NMD, aberrant splicing, ribosomal read-through of stop codons, and protein degradation pathways (ERAD, UPS). However, NMD specifically targets the mRNA transcript itself.

<a id='question-021'></a>

### Question 21: Unambiguous vs Degenerate Nature of the Genetic Code
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The genetic code is unambiguous (each codon specifies only one amino acid) and degenerate (multiple codons can specify the same amino acid). Understanding this principle is crucial for predicting the effects of point mutations on protein structure. (遗传密码是不Ambiguous的（每个密码子只指定一个氨基酸）和Degenerate的（多个密码子可以指定相同的氨基酸）。理解这个原理对于预测点突变对蛋白质结构的影响至关重要。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the clinic complaining of progressive muscle weakness and fatigue, particularly affecting his proximal limb muscles. His symptoms began insidiously over the past year. He denies any history of trauma, infections, or medication use, except for a recent experimental gene therapy trial for a rare genetic disorder. The gene therapy involved introducing a modified viral vector carrying a functional copy of the *SMN1* gene into his motor neurons, aiming to correct a deficiency in Survival Motor Neuron protein. Prior to the trial, he had a confirmed homozygous deletion of exon 7 in both alleles of the *SMN1* gene, leading to Spinal Muscular Atrophy (SMA) type 1. Post-gene therapy, his serum creatine kinase (CK) levels are significantly elevated (15,000 U/L, normal <200 U/L), and electromyography (EMG) shows myopathic changes. Genetic analysis of his muscle biopsy reveals a novel point mutation in the introduced *SMN1* transgene: a single nucleotide substitution in the third position of a codon that normally codes for glycine (Gly). This mutation changes the codon from GGU to AGU. The patient's clinical condition has worsened despite the introduction of the *SMN1* gene.

Which of the following amino acids is most likely encoded by the mutated codon (AGU) in the introduced *SMN1* transgene, explaining the patient's worsening condition?

(A) Alanine
(B) Serine
(C) Threonine
(D) Valine
(E) Aspartic Acid

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old male with progressive proximal muscle weakness and fatigue, suggesting a myopathy or neuromuscular disorder.
- **History:** Experimental gene therapy for SMA type 1 (homozygous deletion of *SMN1* exon 7). SMA is caused by insufficient SMN protein.
- **Post-Therapy Findings:** Elevated CK (muscle damage marker), myopathic EMG changes, worsening clinical condition despite gene therapy. This indicates the introduced *SMN1* transgene is not producing functional SMN protein, or is producing a dysfunctional protein.
- **Genetic Analysis:** Point mutation in the introduced *SMN1* transgene. The codon GGU (coding for Glycine) is changed to AGU.
- **Question:** Identify the amino acid encoded by the mutated codon AGU.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- The genetic code is degenerate, meaning multiple codons can specify the same amino acid.
- The genetic code is unambiguous, meaning a single codon specifies only one amino acid.
- The original codon GGU codes for Glycine (Gly).
- The mutated codon is AGU. We need to determine which amino acid AGU codes for using the standard genetic code table.
- Consulting the genetic code table:
    - GGU codes for Glycine.
    - AGU codes for Serine (Ser).
- The mutation changed a Glycine codon (GGU) to a Serine codon (AGU).
- Glycine is a small, nonpolar amino acid. Serine is a small, polar amino acid.
- This amino acid substitution (Gly -> Ser) likely alters the structure and function of the SMN protein, potentially leading to a non-functional or misfolded protein, explaining the patient's worsening condition despite the presence of the transgene. The introduction of a non-functional protein might even interfere with the function of any residual endogenous SMN protein, or trigger an immune response.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Alanine: Alanine is coded by GCU, GCC, GCA, GCG. AGU does not code for Alanine. This option tests the understanding that a specific codon codes for only one amino acid (unambiguous code).
- (C) Threonine: Threonine is coded by ACU, ACC, ACA, ACG. AGU does not code for Threonine. This option tests the understanding of the specific codons for different amino acids.
- (D) Valine: Valine is coded by GUU, GUC, GUA, GUG. AGU does not code for Valine. This option tests the understanding of the specific codons for different amino acids.
- (E) Aspartic Acid: Aspartic Acid is coded by GAU, GAC. AGU does not code for Aspartic Acid. This option tests the understanding of the specific codons for different amino acids.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- The genetic code is unambiguous (one codon = one amino acid) and degenerate (multiple codons = one amino acid). Point mutations can alter the amino acid sequence, potentially affecting protein function.
- **Differentials:** This question tests the direct application of the genetic code. Other related concepts include frameshift mutations (caused by insertions/deletions not in multiples of 3), silent mutations (changing a codon to another codon coding for the same amino acid), and missense mutations (changing a codon to one coding for a different amino acid). This specific scenario involves a missense mutation due to a single nucleotide substitution in the third codon position (often less impactful due to wobble pairing, but still significant here).

<a id='question-022'></a>

### Question 22: Mitochondrial Translation & Genetic Code Ambiguity
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The mitochondrial genetic code differs from the universal genetic code, particularly in the interpretation of certain stop codons (e.g., UGA codes for tryptophan in mitochondria, not stop). This difference can lead to unexpected protein products when mitochondrial genes are translated. (线粒体遗传密码与通用遗传密码存在差异，特别是某些终止密码子（如UGA）在线粒体中编码色氨酸，而非终止。这种差异可能导致翻译时产生意外的蛋白质产物。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old female presents to the neurology clinic with progressive muscle weakness, exercise intolerance, and recurrent episodes of lactic acidosis. Her symptoms began in her early 20s and have gradually worsened. Family history is significant for similar symptoms in her maternal grandmother. Neurological examination reveals ptosis, ophthalmoplegia, and proximal muscle weakness. Muscle biopsy shows ragged-red fibers on Gomori trichrome stain, indicative of mitochondrial myopathy. Biochemical analysis of muscle tissue reveals a deficiency in Complex IV (cytochrome c oxidase) activity. Genetic testing identifies a homozygous mutation (c.1234A>G) in the mitochondrial DNA (mtDNA) gene encoding subunit IV of cytochrome c oxidase (MT-CO4). This mutation introduces a premature stop codon (UGA) at position 412 within the MT-CO4 mRNA sequence. However, Western blot analysis of muscle tissue shows the presence of a full-length, albeit slightly modified, cytochrome c oxidase subunit IV protein. The patient is being considered for treatment with Eligosertib, an inhibitor of mitochondrial protein synthesis.

Which of the following best explains the presence of the full-length cytochrome c oxidase subunit IV protein despite the premature stop codon mutation in the MT-CO4 gene?

(A) The premature stop codon is bypassed due to ribosomal readthrough facilitated by specific tRNA molecules.
(B) The mutation causes a frameshift, leading to the incorporation of non-standard amino acids downstream of the mutation.
(C) The premature stop codon is recognized as a start codon by the mitochondrial ribosome, leading to translation from an alternative reading frame.
(D) The UGA codon, which normally signals termination in the universal genetic code, codes for tryptophan (Trp) in the mitochondrial genetic code.
(E) The mutation activates an alternative splicing pathway, leading to the exclusion of the mutated exon and the production of a functional protein transcript.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** Young female with progressive muscle weakness, exercise intolerance, lactic acidosis, ptosis, ophthalmoplegia, proximal weakness. These symptoms point towards a mitochondrial myopathy.
- **Family History:** Maternal inheritance pattern (grandmother affected) strongly suggests mitochondrial DNA (mtDNA) inheritance.
- **Muscle Biopsy:** Ragged-red fibers are characteristic of mitochondrial disorders due to abnormal mitochondrial proliferation.
- **Biochemical Analysis:** Deficiency in Complex IV (cytochrome c oxidase) activity confirms a defect in the mitochondrial electron transport chain.
- **Genetic Testing:** Homozygous mutation (c.1234A>G) in MT-CO4 (encoding subunit IV of cytochrome c oxidase) introduces a premature stop codon (UGA) at codon 412.
- **Western Blot:** Presence of a full-length cytochrome c oxidase subunit IV protein despite the premature stop codon is the key finding requiring explanation.
- **Treatment Consideration:** Eligosertib (inhibitor of mitochondrial protein synthesis) is relevant but doesn't explain the protein presence.
- **The Question:** Asks for the mechanism explaining the full-length protein despite the premature stop codon.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- The universal genetic code uses UGA as a stop codon, signaling the termination of translation.
- However, the mitochondrial genetic code, which is derived from bacteria, differs slightly.
- In the mitochondrial genetic code, UGA codes for tryptophan (Trp), not a stop signal.
- The patient has a mutation in MT-CO4, which encodes a subunit of Complex IV.
- The mutation introduces a UGA codon at position 412.
- According to the universal code, this would terminate translation prematurely, resulting in a truncated protein.
- However, because translation occurs within the mitochondria, the mitochondrial genetic code applies.
- In the mitochondria, UGA codes for tryptophan.
- Therefore, the ribosome will incorporate a tRNA carrying tryptophan at this position, allowing translation to continue to the natural stop codon, resulting in a full-length protein.
- The presence of the full-length protein on Western blot confirms that the UGA codon is being read as tryptophan in the mitochondrial translational machinery.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Ribosomal readthrough can occur, especially for premature stop codons, but it's typically mediated by specific factors or tRNA molecules recognizing the stop codon itself, not a specific codon reassignment like UGA->Trp. While readthrough *could* happen, the specific mechanism here is the altered genetic code. This is a plausible mechanism for some premature stop codon issues but not the primary explanation for UGA in mitochondria.
- (B): Frameshifts occur due to insertions or deletions, not point mutations that introduce a stop codon. This mutation is a single nucleotide change (A>G) resulting in a codon change (from presumably an amino acid codon to UGA), not a shift in the reading frame. Therefore, non-standard amino acids downstream are not the explanation.
- (C): The mutation introduces a stop codon (UGA), not a start codon. The mitochondrial ribosome does not typically recognize stop codons as start codons. This mechanism is incorrect.
- (E): Alternative splicing occurs in the nucleus for pre-mRNA, not in mitochondria. Mitochondrial transcripts are processed differently and do not undergo splicing in the same way. Furthermore, the mutation is within the coding sequence, and alternative splicing would involve changes to the intron/exon boundaries or splice sites, not a point mutation within an exon.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Remember that the mitochondrial genetic code is not universal and differs from the standard nuclear genetic code, particularly in the interpretation of UGA (Trp), AGA (Stop), and AGG (Stop). This difference can have significant clinical implications, especially when interpreting mutations in mitochondrial genes.
- **Differentials:** This question tests knowledge of the non-universal nature of the genetic code. Other key concepts related to mitochondrial disorders include maternal inheritance, mitochondrial DNA structure, mitochondrial protein import, and common mitochondrial diseases (e.g., MELAS, MERRF, Leber's Hereditary Optic Neuropathy). Distinguish this from nuclear genetic code mutations, frameshift mutations, and splicing errors.

---

<a id='question-023'></a>

### Question 023: Genetic Code Degeneracy and Frameshift Mutations
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The genetic code is non-overlapping and commaless, meaning each nucleotide is read only once and in groups of three (codons). Frameshift mutations disrupt this reading frame, leading to drastically altered amino acid sequences downstream of the mutation. (遗传密码是非重叠且无逗号的，意味着每个核苷酸只被读取一次，并且以三个一组（密码子）读取。移码突变会破坏这种阅读框架，导致突变下游的氨基酸序列发生巨大变化。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the clinic complaining of progressive muscle weakness and fatigue over the past year. His symptoms began subtly but have worsened, now affecting his ability to walk and perform daily activities. He denies any history of trauma or recent illness. Family history is unremarkable for neuromuscular disorders. Physical examination reveals generalized muscle atrophy, particularly in the proximal limbs, and decreased deep tendon reflexes. Serum creatine kinase (CK) levels are markedly elevated at 15,000 U/L (normal < 200 U/L). Electromyography (EMG) shows myopathic changes. Muscle biopsy reveals abnormal protein aggregates within muscle fibers. Genetic testing reveals a heterozygous mutation in the *DMD* gene, specifically a single base pair deletion (guanine) at nucleotide position 5432 within exon 51. This deletion causes a frameshift mutation. The normal sequence at this position is ...-G-A-T-C-A-T-G-G-C-..., and the mutated sequence is ...-G-A-T-C-A-T-G-G-C-.... The protein product of the wild-type *DMD* gene is dystrophin, a large cytoskeletal protein crucial for muscle fiber integrity. Analysis of the mutated dystrophin protein shows a truncated protein lacking the C-terminal domain, which is essential for interaction with the cytoskeleton.

Which of the following molecular consequences is MOST directly responsible for the patient's phenotype resulting from the identified *DMD* gene mutation?

(A) Introduction of a premature stop codon leading to nonsense-mediated mRNA decay.
(B) Alteration of the amino acid sequence downstream of the mutation due to the frameshift, resulting in a non-functional protein.
(C) Splicing defect causing exon skipping and deletion of a critical functional domain.
(D) Activation of the unfolded protein response (UPR) due to misfolding of the truncated protein.
(E) Increased degradation of the mutated mRNA by cellular exonucleases.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old male with progressive muscle weakness, fatigue, muscle atrophy, decreased reflexes, elevated CK, myopathic EMG, and abnormal protein aggregates in muscle biopsy. This clinical picture is highly suggestive of a muscular dystrophy.
- **Genetic Finding:** Heterozygous mutation in the *DMD* gene (associated with Duchenne/Becker muscular dystrophy). The specific mutation is a single base pair deletion (Guanine) at nucleotide position 5432 within exon 51.
- **Molecular Consequence:** The deletion causes a frameshift mutation. The normal reading frame is disrupted.
- **Protein Product:** The mutated dystrophin protein is truncated, lacking the C-terminal domain, which is essential for its function.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST direct molecular consequence responsible for the patient's phenotype.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- The genetic code is read in triplets (codons).
- It is non-overlapping: Each nucleotide is part of only one codon.
- It is commaless: There are no intervening nucleotides between codons.
- The *DMD* gene mutation is a single base pair deletion (G) at position 5432 within exon 51.
- Normal sequence: ...-G-A-T-C-A-T-G-G-C-...
- Mutated sequence: ...-G-A-T-C-A-T-G-G-C-...
- The deletion shifts the reading frame. Let's assume the normal reading frame starts before this point.
    - Normal: ...-GAT-CAT-GGC-... (Codon 1, Codon 2, Codon 3...)
    - Mutated: ...-GAT-CAT-GGG-C-... (Codon 1, Codon 2, Codon 3...)
- The deletion of 'G' causes all subsequent codons to be read incorrectly. For example, the codon 'CAT' becomes 'CA', and the next codon 'GGC' becomes 'TG'.
- This frameshift leads to the incorporation of entirely different amino acids downstream of the mutation site.
- The resulting protein is significantly altered in sequence, often leading to premature stop codons further downstream or a completely non-functional protein due to the drastic change in amino acid sequence.
- In this case, the mutation leads to a truncated protein lacking the C-terminal domain, confirming the severe alteration in the protein sequence caused by the frameshift.
- The patient's phenotype (muscular dystrophy) is directly caused by the lack of functional dystrophin protein.
- Therefore, the frameshift mutation altering the amino acid sequence downstream is the most direct cause of the non-functional protein and the resulting phenotype.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Introduction of a premature stop codon leading to nonsense-mediated mRNA decay. While frameshift mutations *can* introduce premature stop codons, the vignette specifically states the protein is truncated but doesn't explicitly mention a premature stop codon *within* the mutated sequence shown or that the mRNA undergoes decay. The primary consequence described is the altered amino acid sequence leading to a non-functional, truncated protein. Nonsense-mediated decay is a possible secondary effect but not the *most direct* cause of the altered protein structure and function described.
- (C): Splicing defect causing exon skipping and deletion of a critical functional domain. The mutation is described as a deletion *within* an exon (exon 51), not at an exon-intron boundary. While mutations near splice sites can cause splicing defects, this specific mutation is a frameshift within the coding sequence of the exon itself.
- (D): Activation of the unfolded protein response (UPR) due to misfolding of the truncated protein. The UPR is activated when misfolded proteins accumulate in the endoplasmic reticulum. While the truncated dystrophin might misfold and potentially trigger UPR, the *primary* molecular consequence of the frameshift mutation itself is the alteration of the amino acid sequence, leading to the non-functional protein. UPR is a cellular response to the misfolded protein, not the direct result of the frameshift altering the code.
- (E): Increased degradation of the mutated mRNA by cellular exonucleases. While cellular quality control mechanisms might target aberrant mRNA, the primary defect is the altered reading frame and resulting protein sequence. The mRNA itself might be translated into the altered protein before potential degradation. The frameshift's direct impact is on the protein sequence, not primarily on mRNA stability.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- The genetic code is non-overlapping and commaless. Frameshift mutations (insertions or deletions not in multiples of three) disrupt the reading frame, leading to a completely different amino acid sequence downstream of the mutation. This typically results in a non-functional protein.
- Differential: Missense mutations change a single amino acid, nonsense mutations introduce a premature stop codon, silent mutations do not change the amino acid sequence. Frameshift mutations cause the most drastic alteration in protein sequence.

<a id='question-024'></a>

### Question 24: Wobble Hypothesis (Crick): Non-Standard Base Pairing at Codon 3rd Position / Anticodon 1st Position - Clinical Scenario 2
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The wobble hypothesis describes non-standard base pairing between the third nucleotide of a codon and the first nucleotide of the anticodon, allowing a single tRNA to recognize multiple codons. This flexibility is crucial for efficient translation. (沃勃尔假说描述了密码子第三个核苷酸和反密码子第一个核苷酸之间的非标准配对，允许单个tRNA识别多个密码子。这种灵活性对于有效的翻译至关重要。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the emergency department with acute onset of severe muscle weakness, respiratory distress, and cardiac arrhythmias. He has a history of intravenous drug abuse. Initial laboratory tests reveal markedly elevated serum creatine kinase (CK) levels (25,000 U/L), hyperkalemia (K+ = 7.2 mEq/L), and dark, tea-colored urine. A urine sample is analyzed for myoglobinuria. Genetic testing reveals a heterozygous mutation in the *GSTM1* gene, resulting in a complete absence of the GSTM1 enzyme. The patient is treated with intravenous fluids and supportive care. Further investigation reveals that the patient was exposed to a toxic substance metabolized by GSTM1. This substance, normally detoxified by GSTM1 conjugation with glutathione, accumulates and causes oxidative damage, particularly affecting muscle tissue. The toxic substance is structurally similar to a known carcinogen, but its specific identity is unknown. The patient's condition improves with treatment, but he is advised to avoid further exposure to potential toxins metabolized by GSTM1. The toxic substance is known to be a three-carbon electrophile that reacts with a nucleophile on a protein. The nucleophile is the sulfur atom of a cysteine residue. The toxic substance is metabolized by GSTM1 to a less toxic, water-soluble compound via conjugation with glutathione. The specific codon for the amino acid cysteine is UGU or UGC. The anticodon of the tRNA responsible for recognizing these codons must therefore be able to pair with both U and C at the wobble position (the first position of the anticodon).

Which of the following anticodon sequences is most likely responsible for recognizing both UGU and UGC codons for cysteine?

(A) 5'-ICA-3' (where I = Inosine)
(B) 5'-AGU-3'
(C) 5'-ACG-3'
(D) 5'-UGA-3'
(E) 5'-AGC-3'

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** Young male, IV drug abuse, acute muscle weakness, respiratory distress, arrhythmias, high CK, hyperkalemia, myoglobinuria. This points towards rhabdomyolysis, likely due to toxic exposure.
- **Genetic Finding:** Heterozygous *GSTM1* mutation leading to enzyme absence. GSTM1 is a detoxification enzyme.
- **Toxic Exposure:** Patient was exposed to a toxin normally detoxified by GSTM1. The toxin causes oxidative damage, especially in muscle.
- **Toxin Mechanism:** A three-carbon electrophile reacting with cysteine thiol groups. GSTM1 conjugates it with glutathione for detoxification.
- **Biochemical Question:** The question asks for the anticodon sequence that recognizes both UGU and UGC codons for cysteine, linking the clinical scenario to the wobble hypothesis.
- **Key Clue:** The codons for cysteine are UGU and UGC. The question asks for the anticodon that recognizes *both*. This implies wobble pairing at the third codon position (U or C) with the first anticodon position.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Wobble Hypothesis (Crick):** Proposed that the pairing rules between the third base of a codon and the first base of the anticodon are less strict than Watson-Crick base pairing. This allows a single tRNA molecule to recognize more than one codon.
- **Cysteine Codons:** The codons for cysteine are UGU and UGC.
- **Anticodon Pairing:** The anticodon must pair with the codon in an antiparallel fashion.
    - Codon 1: UGU
    - Codon 2: UGC
- **First Position of Anticodon:** The first base of the anticodon pairs with the third base of the codon. Therefore, the anticodon must have a base that can pair with both U and C at the wobble position.
- **Wobble Rules:** According to Crick's wobble rules, Inosine (I) is a modified nucleoside found in the first position of some tRNAs. Inosine can base pair with U, C, and A.
- **Anticodon Structure:** The anticodon reads 5' to 3', while the codon reads 5' to 3'.
    - For UGU, the anticodon is 3'-ACG-5' (read 5'-GCA-3').
    - For UGC, the anticodon is 3'-ACG-5' (read 5'-GCA-3').
- **Wobble Application:** The first base of the anticodon (pairing with the third base of the codon) must be able to wobble pair with both U and C. Inosine (I) is the classic wobble base that pairs with U, C, or A. Therefore, an anticodon of the form 5'-ICA-3' (where I = Inosine) can recognize both UGU and UGC codons. The 'CA' part represents the standard Watson-Crick pairing with the second codon base (G) and the first codon base (U).
- **GST Enzymes:** Glutathione S-transferases (GSTs) catalyze the conjugation of glutathione to electrophilic compounds, facilitating their detoxification and excretion. GSTM1 is a specific isoform. Deficiency increases susceptibility to toxins.
- **Clinical Link:** The patient's rhabdomyolysis is caused by a toxin that GSTM1 normally detoxifies. The toxin is an electrophile reacting with cysteine. The question tests the molecular mechanism (wobble hypothesis) related to the amino acid cysteine.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **5'-ICA-3' (where I = Inosine)**: This is the correct answer. Inosine (I) at the first position of the anticodon can wobble pair with U, C, or A. Since the codons for cysteine are UGU and UGC, the anticodon must pair with U at the wobble position (third codon base) and C at the wobble position. Inosine fulfills this requirement. The rest of the anticodon (CA) pairs with the G and U of the codon, respectively.
- (B): **5'-AGU-3'**: This anticodon would pair with the codon UCA. This codon codes for serine. This anticodon cannot recognize both UGU and UGC. The first base 'A' pairs specifically with U, and the third base 'U' pairs specifically with A.
- (C): **5'-ACG-3'**: This anticodon would pair with the codon CGU. This codon codes for arginine. This anticodon cannot recognize both UGU and UGC. The first base 'A' pairs specifically with U, and the third base 'G' pairs specifically with C.
- (D): **5'-UGA-3'**: This anticodon would pair with the codon UGA. This codon is a stop codon. This anticodon cannot recognize both UGU and UGC. The first base 'U' pairs specifically with A, and the third base 'A' pairs specifically with U.
- (E): **5'-AGC-3'**: This anticodon would pair with the codon GCA. This codon codes for alanine. This anticodon cannot recognize both UGU and UGC. The first base 'A' pairs specifically with U, and the third base 'C' pairs specifically with G.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** The wobble hypothesis explains how a single tRNA can recognize multiple codons, particularly when the third base of the codon varies. Inosine (I) is the key wobble base that can pair with U, C, or A. Understanding this is crucial for interpreting genetic code redundancy and its implications.
- **Differentials:** This question tests knowledge of the wobble hypothesis. Other related concepts include standard Watson-Crick base pairing (A-U, G-C), codon degeneracy, tRNA structure and function, and the genetic code. It also links to enzyme function (GSTs) and toxicology.

<a id='question-025'></a>

### Question 025: tRNA Wobble Hypothesis & Genetic Code Ambiguity
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The wobble hypothesis explains how a single tRNA anticodon can recognize multiple mRNA codons, particularly at the third (wobble) position, due to non-standard base pairing involving inosine (I). This allows the genetic code to be more efficient. (I-密码子摆动假说解释了单个tRNA反密码子如何识别多个mRNA密码子，特别是在第三位（摆动位点），这归因于涉及肌苷（I）的非标准碱基配对。这使得遗传密码更加高效。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the clinic complaining of progressive muscle weakness and fatigue, particularly affecting proximal muscles. His symptoms began insidiously over the past year. Family history is significant for a similar condition in his maternal grandmother. Neurological examination reveals ptosis, ophthalmoplegia, and proximal muscle weakness (4/5 strength in upper and lower extremities). Serum creatine kinase (CK) levels are markedly elevated at 15,000 U/L (normal < 200 U/L). Electromyography (EMG) shows myopathic changes. Muscle biopsy reveals ragged red fibers on Gomori trichrome stain, suggestive of mitochondrial myopathy. Genetic testing reveals a homozygous mutation in the *MT-TL1* gene, which encodes the tRNA<sup>Leu(UUR)</sup> gene located in the mitochondrial genome. This mutation alters the anticodon sequence from AGU to AGU, but specifically affects the modification of uridine (U) at the wobble position (position 5) of the tRNA anticodon, preventing its conversion to inosine (I). This tRNA<sup>Leu(UUR)</sup> normally decodes the codon UUR (leucine) and also recognizes the stop codons UAG, UGA, and UAA due to wobble pairing rules. The patient's mutation impairs the tRNA's ability to recognize these stop codons. Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the patient's phenotype?

(A) The mutation causes a frameshift in the mitochondrial mRNA, leading to premature termination of protein synthesis for all mitochondrial proteins.
(B) The mutation prevents the proper charging of the tRNA<sup>Leu(UUR)</sup> with leucine, leading to a deficiency in leucine incorporation into mitochondrial proteins.
(C) The mutation impairs the ability of the tRNA<sup>Leu(UUR)</sup> to recognize and decode the stop codons UAG, UGA, and UAA, leading to readthrough of these codons and synthesis of elongated mitochondrial proteins.
(D) The mutation causes misincorporation of adenosine (A) instead of uridine (U) at the wobble position of the tRNA anticodon, leading to the decoding of inappropriate codons.
(E) The mutation leads to the accumulation of uncharged tRNA<sup>Leu(UUR)</sup> in the mitochondria, which inhibits the function of other mitochondrial tRNAs.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old male with progressive proximal muscle weakness, ptosis, ophthalmoplegia, high CK, myopathic EMG, ragged red fibers on biopsy. This points towards a mitochondrial myopathy.
- **Family History:** Maternal inheritance pattern (maternal grandmother affected) strongly suggests a mitochondrial DNA (mtDNA) mutation.
- **Genetic Testing:** Homozygous mutation in *MT-TL1* gene, which encodes tRNA<sup>Leu(UUR)</sup>. This confirms a mitochondrial genetic defect affecting a specific tRNA.
- **Mutation Details:** The mutation affects the modification of uridine (U) at the wobble position (5th position) of the tRNA anticodon, preventing its conversion to inosine (I).
- **Normal Function of tRNA<sup>Leu(UUR)</sup>:** This tRNA normally decodes UUR (leucine) and, due to wobble pairing, also recognizes the stop codons UAG, UGA, and UAA.
- **Wobble Hypothesis:** Inosine (I) in the wobble position of a tRNA anticodon can base pair with U, C, or A in the third position of an mRNA codon. The anticodon sequence AGU (which becomes AGI after U modification to I) can pair with UAG, UAA, UAC, UGU, UGC, and UCG. However, the key function here is its recognition of stop codons.
- **Consequence of Mutation:** Preventing U to I modification means the anticodon remains AGU. AGU cannot effectively pair with UAG, UGA, or UAA according to standard Watson-Crick rules (G pairs with C, U pairs with A). Therefore, the tRNA<sup>Leu(UUR)</sup> loses its ability to recognize these stop codons.
- **Phenotype Explanation:** Loss of stop codon recognition leads to "readthrough" – the ribosome continues translation beyond the intended stop codon, synthesizing elongated proteins. This results in non-functional or abnormally functioning mitochondrial proteins, particularly those encoded by mtDNA (like subunits of oxidative phosphorylation complexes), leading to mitochondrial dysfunction and myopathy (MELAS - Mitochondrial Encephalopathy, Lactic Acidosis, and Stroke-like episodes, often associated with *MT-TL1* mutations).

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Genetic Code & tRNA:** The genetic code is degenerate, meaning multiple codons can specify the same amino acid. tRNA molecules are responsible for bringing the correct amino acid to the ribosome based on the mRNA codon.
- **Anticodon:** Each tRNA has an anticodon loop that base pairs with the mRNA codon.
- **Wobble Hypothesis (Crick):** Proposed that the base pairing rules at the third codon position (wobble position) are less strict. This allows a single tRNA to recognize multiple codons.
- **Inosine (I):** A modified nucleoside found in tRNA. It is formed by the deamination of adenosine (A). Inosine in the wobble position (e.g., anticodon 5'-AGI-3') can pair with U, C, or A in the codon (e.g., codon 5'-XUA-3', 5'-XUC-3', 5'-XUA-3').
- **tRNA<sup>Leu(UUR)</sup> Function:** In mitochondria, tRNA<sup>Leu(UUR)</sup> has the anticodon AGU. Post-transcriptionally, the U at the wobble position is modified to inosine (I), changing the anticodon to AGI. This modified tRNA<sup>Leu(UUR)</sup> can then recognize the leucine codon UUR and also the stop codons UAG, UGA, and UAA due to the wobble pairing properties of inosine.
- **Mechanism of Disease:** The mutation in *MT-TL1* prevents the conversion of U to I at the wobble position. The tRNA anticodon remains AGU. AGU cannot pair with UAG, UGA, or UAA. Consequently, the ribosome fails to recognize these stop codons, leading to readthrough and the synthesis of elongated mitochondrial proteins. These elongated proteins are often non-functional, disrupting mitochondrial respiratory chain complexes and causing mitochondrial dysfunction, manifesting as myopathy, encephalopathy, etc. (MELAS syndrome).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** The mutation affects a specific tRNA's anticodon modification, not the mRNA sequence itself. Frameshifts occur due to insertions or deletions in the mRNA sequence, leading to altered reading frames downstream. This mutation doesn't cause a frameshift.
- (B): **Incorrect.** While the tRNA is involved in protein synthesis, the primary defect is not the charging of the tRNA with leucine. The tRNA is likely still charged with leucine. The problem lies in its inability to recognize stop codons due to the lack of wobble modification (U to I). A deficiency in leucine incorporation would lead to a general lack of leucine in proteins, not specifically elongated mitochondrial proteins.
- (D): **Incorrect.** The mutation prevents the conversion of U to I, not the incorporation of A instead of U. If A were incorporated, the anticodon would be AGU (if U was replaced by A) or AGA (if G was replaced by A), which would decode different codons and potentially cause misincorporation of amino acids, but this is not the mechanism described. The specific defect is the *lack* of I formation from U.
- (E): **Incorrect.** The mutation affects the function of a *specific* tRNA (tRNA<sup>Leu(UUR)</sup>), not the accumulation of uncharged tRNA. While mitochondrial diseases can involve tRNA dysfunction, the primary issue here is the loss of stop codon recognition due to the lack of wobble modification, leading to readthrough, not inhibition by uncharged tRNA.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **Takeaway:** Understand the wobble hypothesis and the role of modified bases like inosine (I) in tRNA anticodons, particularly their ability to recognize multiple codons, including stop codons. Recognize that mutations affecting tRNA genes, especially those involved in wobble modification, can lead to mitochondrial diseases by disrupting protein synthesis termination.
- **Differentials:** Other mitochondrial myopathies (e.g., caused by mutations in mtDNA genes encoding respiratory chain subunits), other causes of myopathy (e.g., muscular dystrophies, inflammatory myopathies), other tRNA mutations affecting amino acid decoding. This specific scenario highlights the unique role of tRNA<sup>Leu(UUR)</sup> in recognizing stop codons in mitochondria.

<a id='question-026'></a>

### Question 26: Start Codon (AUG): Specifying N-Formylmethionine (fMet) in Bacteria vs Methionine in Eukaryotes - Clinical Scenario 2
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The initiation of translation differs between prokaryotes and eukaryotes, specifically regarding the initiator tRNA and the first amino acid incorporated. Prokaryotes use N-formylmethionine (fMet) initiated by a formylated tRNA carrying methionine, while eukaryotes use methionine (Met) initiated by a non-formylated tRNA carrying methionine. This difference is exploited in antibiotic targeting. (翻译起始过程的差异：原核生物使用N-甲酰甲硫氨酸(fMet)，真核生物使用甲硫氨酸(Met)。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to the emergency department with severe dyspnea, chest pain, and a productive cough with purulent sputum. He has a history of chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) and recent hospitalization for community-acquired pneumonia. His vital signs show a temperature of 38.5°C, heart rate of 110 bpm, respiratory rate of 28 breaths/min, and blood pressure of 90/60 mmHg. Physical examination reveals bilateral crackles and wheezing. A chest X-ray shows extensive bilateral infiltrates. Sputum culture grows *Pseudomonas aeruginosa*, which is identified as resistant to multiple antibiotics, including piperacillin-tazobactam and ceftazidime. The microbiology lab reports that the strain possesses a gene encoding a modified elongation factor Tu (EF-Tu) that binds GTP analogs with significantly reduced affinity. Further investigation reveals that this modified EF-Tu protein is insensitive to the inhibitory effects of cycloheximide. The patient is subsequently treated with a combination of colistin and tobramycin, which proves effective.

Which of the following mechanisms BEST explains why cycloheximide is ineffective against *Pseudomonas aeruginosa* but effective against eukaryotic cells?

(A) Cycloheximide inhibits prokaryotic DNA gyrase, preventing DNA replication, while it inhibits eukaryotic topoisomerase II, preventing transcription.
(B) Cycloheximide inhibits prokaryotic ribosomal RNA synthesis by blocking RNA polymerase, while it inhibits eukaryotic ribosomal RNA synthesis by blocking RNA polymerase II.
(C) Cycloheximide inhibits prokaryotic initiation factor eIF2, preventing the binding of initiator tRNA to the ribosome, while it inhibits eukaryotic initiation factor eIF2, preventing the binding of initiator tRNA to the ribosome.
(D) Cycloheximide inhibits prokaryotic peptidyl transferase activity, preventing peptide bond formation, while it inhibits eukaryotic peptidyl transferase activity, preventing peptide bond formation.
(E) Cycloheximide inhibits prokaryotic elongation factor Tu (EF-Tu), preventing the binding of aminoacyl-tRNA to the A-site of the ribosome, while it inhibits eukaryotic elongation factor 2 (EF-2), preventing translocation.

#### Correct Answer: E

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- The patient presents with severe pneumonia caused by *Pseudomonas aeruginosa*.
- The bacteria are resistant to common antibiotics.
- The resistance mechanism involves a modified elongation factor Tu (EF-Tu) insensitive to cycloheximide.
- The question asks why cycloheximide is ineffective against *Pseudomonas aeruginosa* (prokaryote) but effective against eukaryotes.
- This points to a difference in the target or mechanism of action of cycloheximide between prokaryotes and eukaryotes.
- The key clue is the modified EF-Tu in *Pseudomonas* being insensitive to cycloheximide, implying cycloheximide targets EF-Tu or a related process, but the prokaryotic version is different enough or the drug doesn't bind effectively.
- We need to identify the correct target of cycloheximide and how it differs between prokaryotes and eukaryotes.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- Cycloheximide is an antibiotic that inhibits protein synthesis.
- It specifically targets the elongation step of translation.
- In eukaryotes, cycloheximide binds to the peptidyl transferase center of the 60S ribosomal subunit, preventing the binding of the aminoacyl-tRNA to the A-site. This action is mediated by inhibiting eukaryotic elongation factor 2 (eEF2), which is required for the translocation step (movement of the ribosome along the mRNA). While the primary binding site is the 60S subunit, its effect is often described in relation to eEF2 function.
- In prokaryotes (like *Pseudomonas aeruginosa*), the elongation factor responsible for bringing aminoacyl-tRNAs to the A-site is elongation factor Tu (EF-Tu). EF-Tu binds GTP and an aminoacyl-tRNA and delivers it to the A-site. Hydrolysis of GTP releases EF-Tu-GDP, allowing the tRNA to bind.
- Cycloheximide does *not* bind to the prokaryotic ribosome (EF-Tu or the 70S ribosome) with high affinity and therefore does not inhibit prokaryotic protein synthesis effectively.
- The vignette states the modified EF-Tu in *Pseudomonas* is insensitive to cycloheximide, confirming that cycloheximide's target is related to elongation factors, but the specific prokaryotic target (EF-Tu) is not effectively inhibited by this drug.
- Therefore, cycloheximide inhibits eukaryotic EF-2 (or the 60S subunit interaction related to EF-2 function/tRNA binding), while it does not effectively inhibit prokaryotic EF-Tu.
- Option (E) correctly identifies the targets: prokaryotic EF-Tu and eukaryotic EF-2.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Cycloheximide does not inhibit DNA gyrase (prokaryotic) or topoisomerase II (eukaryotic). These enzymes are involved in DNA replication and transcription, not translation elongation. This option confuses cycloheximide's target with targets of quinolones (DNA gyrase) or etoposide (topoisomerase II).
- (B): Cycloheximide does not inhibit RNA polymerase (prokaryotic or eukaryotic) or ribosomal RNA synthesis. RNA polymerase is involved in transcription. This option confuses cycloheximide's target with targets of rifampicin (prokaryotic RNA polymerase) or α-amanitin (eukaryotic RNA polymerase II).
- (C): Cycloheximide does not inhibit initiation factor eIF2 (eukaryotic) or its prokaryotic equivalent (IF2). eIF2 is involved in the initiation complex formation, bringing the initiator tRNA (Met-tRNAi) to the ribosome. Cycloheximide targets elongation.
- (D): Cycloheximide does not inhibit peptidyl transferase activity directly. Peptidyl transferase is an activity of the large ribosomal subunit (23S rRNA in prokaryotes, 28S rRNA in eukaryotes) and catalyzes peptide bond formation. While cycloheximide binds near the peptidyl transferase center in eukaryotes, its primary mechanism involves preventing aminoacyl-tRNA binding to the A-site via EF-2 inhibition/interaction. Chloramphenicol and lincomycin are examples of drugs that inhibit peptidyl transferase.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- Cycloheximide is a specific inhibitor of eukaryotic protein synthesis, targeting the elongation step (specifically EF-2 or the 60S subunit interaction). It is ineffective against prokaryotes because it does not bind effectively to prokaryotic ribosomes or elongation factors like EF-Tu. This difference in target specificity is a key concept in pharmacology and molecular biology.
- Differentials: Chloramphenicol inhibits peptidyl transferase in both prokaryotes and eukaryotes. Tetracyclines inhibit prokaryotic A-site binding. Aminoglycosides cause misreading of mRNA in prokaryotes. Erythromycin inhibits prokaryotic translocation (EF-G). Rifampicin inhibits prokaryotic RNA polymerase.

<a id='question-027'></a>

### Question 27: Stop Codons (UAA, UAG, UGA): Non-Coding Termination Signals Recognized by Release Factors - Clinical Scenario 2
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of stop codons (UAA, UAG, UGA) in translation termination and the mechanism by which release factors recognize these codons, leading to polypeptide chain release. (理解终止密码子（UAA、UAG、UGA）在翻译终止中的作用以及释放因子识别这些密码子的机制，从而导致多肽链释放。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male presents to the emergency department with acute onset of severe muscle weakness, respiratory distress, and dysphagia. His symptoms began approximately 2 hours after consuming a home-canned meal. He reports feeling tingling sensations in his extremities prior to the onset of weakness. Vital signs show a respiratory rate of 32 breaths/min, heart rate of 110 bpm, and blood pressure of 90/60 mmHg. Physical examination reveals flaccid paralysis, ptosis, and dilated pupils. Laboratory tests show normal serum electrolytes, glucose, and renal function. Arterial blood gas analysis reveals respiratory acidosis (pH 7.28, PaCO2 65 mmHg, PaO2 70 mmHg on room air). A sample of the home-canned meal is sent for analysis. Subsequent analysis of the meal reveals the presence of a potent neurotoxin, specifically identified as a bacterial exotoxin that inhibits the function of elongation factor 2 (EF-2). This toxin structurally mimics an aminoacyl-tRNA and binds to the A-site of the ribosome, preventing the translocation step of translation. Which of the following molecular events is most directly inhibited by this bacterial exotoxin, leading to the patient's symptoms?

(A) The binding of initiator tRNA to the small ribosomal subunit.
(B) The recognition of start codons (AUG) by the ribosome.
(C) The release of the completed polypeptide chain from the ribosome.
(D) The binding of aminoacyl-tRNAs to the A-site of the ribosome.
(E) The formation of peptide bonds between amino acids.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** Acute severe muscle weakness, respiratory distress, dysphagia, tingling sensations, flaccid paralysis, ptosis, dilated pupils, respiratory acidosis. This constellation of symptoms points towards a neuromuscular blockade, specifically affecting acetylcholine release at the neuromuscular junction.
*   **History:** Symptoms onset after consuming home-canned food suggests botulism, caused by *Clostridium botulinum* toxin.
*   **Lab Findings:** Respiratory acidosis confirms respiratory muscle paralysis.
*   **Toxin Mechanism:** The vignette explicitly states the toxin inhibits elongation factor 2 (EF-2) function. EF-2 is crucial for the translocation step in eukaryotic translation. The toxin mimics aminoacyl-tRNA and binds to the A-site, blocking translocation.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular event *most directly inhibited* by the toxin's effect on EF-2, leading to the symptoms.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **Translation Process:** Protein synthesis (translation) involves initiation, elongation, and termination.
    *   **Initiation:** Ribosome binds mRNA, initiator tRNA (carrying methionine) binds the start codon (AUG) in the P-site.
    *   **Elongation:** Aminoacyl-tRNAs bind to the A-site (codon-anticodon pairing), peptide bond formation (peptidyl transferase activity of the large ribosomal subunit), and translocation (EF-2 dependent in eukaryotes, EF-G dependent in prokaryotes). Translocation moves the peptidyl-tRNA from the A-site to the P-site, and the empty tRNA from the P-site to the E-site, opening the A-site for the next aminoacyl-tRNA.
    *   **Termination:** When a stop codon (UAA, UAG, UGA) enters the A-site, it is recognized by release factors (eRF1 in eukaryotes, RF1/RF2/RF3 in prokaryotes). These factors do not carry amino acids but bind to the stop codon and promote hydrolysis of the bond between the polypeptide chain and the tRNA in the P-site, releasing the completed polypeptide. GTP hydrolysis by the release factor provides the energy for this process.
*   **Toxin Mechanism & Inhibition:** The bacterial exotoxin (botulinum toxin) inhibits EF-2. EF-2 is essential for the translocation step. By inhibiting EF-2, the ribosome cannot move along the mRNA after peptide bond formation. This stalls translation.
*   **Connecting Inhibition to Symptoms:** Stalled translation prevents the synthesis of essential proteins, including those required for neurotransmitter release (like acetylcholine at the neuromuscular junction). This leads to flaccid paralysis, respiratory failure, and other symptoms described in the vignette.
*   **Analyzing the Options:**
    *   (A) Initiator tRNA binding: Occurs during initiation, not directly affected by EF-2 inhibition during elongation.
    *   (B) Start codon recognition: Occurs during initiation, not directly affected by EF-2 inhibition during elongation.
    *   (C) Polypeptide chain release: Occurs during termination. While EF-2 inhibition stalls elongation, the *ultimate* consequence of stalled elongation is that the ribosome cannot reach stop codons, preventing termination and release of *newly synthesized* proteins. The question asks what is *most directly inhibited* by the toxin's effect on EF-2. EF-2 inhibition directly blocks translocation, which is part of elongation. However, the *functional outcome* relevant to the symptoms is the failure to produce necessary proteins, which requires termination. The inhibition of EF-2 prevents the ribosome from reaching the stop codon, thus preventing the release of the polypeptide chain. This is the most direct consequence leading to the lack of functional proteins.
    *   (D) Aminoacyl-tRNA binding to A-site: This is part of elongation, but the toxin binds to the A-site *after* aminoacyl-tRNA binding (or prevents the next aminoacyl-tRNA from binding) and blocks translocation. The primary inhibition is translocation, not the initial binding.
    *   (E) Peptide bond formation: This is catalyzed by the ribosome (peptidyl transferase) and occurs *before* translocation. EF-2 inhibition occurs *after* peptide bond formation.

*   **Refined Analysis of (C):** The toxin inhibits EF-2, which is required for translocation. Translocation moves the ribosome along the mRNA. If translocation is blocked, the ribosome cannot reach the next codon, including stop codons. Therefore, the release of the polypeptide chain is prevented because the ribosome never gets to the termination step. This is the most direct consequence of blocking translocation that prevents the synthesis of functional proteins needed for neuromuscular function.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) The binding of initiator tRNA to the small ribosomal subunit: This is part of the initiation phase of translation. The toxin inhibits EF-2, which functions during the elongation phase (translocation). Therefore, this option is incorrect. Students might confuse the different phases of translation.
- (B) The recognition of start codons (AUG) by the ribosome: This also occurs during the initiation phase. The toxin's effect is on elongation. Therefore, this option is incorrect. Students might focus on the ribosome's general function without considering the specific phase affected.
- (D) The binding of aminoacyl-tRNAs to the A-site of the ribosome: This is the first step of the elongation cycle, occurring *before* the step inhibited by EF-2 (translocation). While the toxin binds to the A-site, its primary mechanism is blocking translocation, not the initial binding of the tRNA. Therefore, this option is incorrect. Students might focus on the A-site binding without understanding the precise step inhibited by EF-2.
- (E) The formation of peptide bonds between amino acids: This is the core reaction of elongation, catalyzed by the ribosome's peptidyl transferase activity, occurring *before* translocation. EF-2 is involved in translocation, which happens *after* peptide bond formation. Therefore, this option is incorrect. Students might confuse the order of events in the elongation cycle.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The key takeaway is understanding the role of elongation factors, specifically EF-2, in the translocation step of eukaryotic translation. Inhibition of translocation prevents the ribosome from moving along the mRNA, ultimately blocking the synthesis of proteins required for cellular function. This mechanism is exemplified by botulinum toxin. Differential diagnosis includes other toxins affecting neuromuscular junctions (e.g., tetanus toxin, curare), but the specific mechanism described points directly to translation inhibition via EF-2. Understanding the steps of translation (initiation, elongation, termination) and the factors involved is crucial.

<a id='question-028'></a>

### Question 28: Point Mutations: Transitions vs Transversions - Clinical Scenario 2
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Differentiating between transition (purine ↔ purine or pyrimidine ↔ pyrimidine) and transversion (purine ↔ pyrimidine) point mutations based on their effect on protein structure and function, specifically in the context of enzyme activity and substrate specificity. (区分过渡突变（嘌呤↔嘌呤或嘧啶↔嘧啶）和横向突变（嘌呤↔嘧啶）对蛋白质结构和功能的影响，特别是在酶活性和底物特异性的背景下。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to his physician complaining of severe, progressive muscle weakness and fatigue, particularly after exercise. He reports a history of recurrent episodes of dark urine (myoglobinuria) following strenuous activity since adolescence. His father had similar symptoms and died unexpectedly at age 45 during a soccer game. Physical examination reveals generalized muscle atrophy and tenderness. Laboratory tests show markedly elevated serum creatine kinase (CK) levels (over 10,000 U/L, normal <200 U/L) and significantly reduced levels of ATP in muscle biopsies. Genetic testing reveals a homozygous mutation in the *IMPDH1* gene, which encodes inosine monophosphate dehydrogenase. Sequencing of the patient's *IMPDH1* gene shows a single nucleotide change at position 342, where a guanine (G) has been replaced by an adenine (A). The wild-type sequence at this position is G-C.

Which type of point mutation is responsible for the patient's condition?

(A) Transversion
(B) Transition
(C) Frameshift mutation
(D) Nonsense mutation
(E) Silent mutation

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old male with progressive muscle weakness, fatigue, myoglobinuria after exercise, muscle atrophy, and tenderness. These symptoms suggest a myopathy, likely related to energy metabolism defects in muscle.
- **Family History:** Father had similar symptoms and died young during exercise, suggesting an inherited disorder, possibly autosomal recessive given the severity and early onset.
- **Lab Findings:** Markedly elevated CK indicates muscle damage. Reduced ATP levels in muscle biopsies point towards a defect in ATP production or utilization.
- **Genetic Findings:** Homozygous mutation in *IMPDH1* gene. *IMPDH1* encodes inosine monophosphate dehydrogenase, a key enzyme in the *de novo* synthesis of guanine nucleotides (GMP). This enzyme is crucial for purine biosynthesis.
- **Specific Mutation:** Single nucleotide change at position 342: Guanine (G) replaced by Adenine (A). The wild-type sequence is G-C.
- **Question:** Identify the type of point mutation.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **IMPDH1 Function:** Inosine monophosphate dehydrogenase (IMPDH) catalyzes the conversion of inosine monophosphate (IMP) to xanthosine monophosphate (XMP), which is then hydrolyzed to guanosine monophosphate (GMP). GMP is a precursor for guanine nucleotides (GDP, GTP, dGDP, dGTP), essential for DNA/RNA synthesis and energy metabolism (GTP).
- **Purine Biosynthesis:** The *de novo* pathway synthesizes purine nucleotides. IMP is a key branch point intermediate. IMPDH converts IMP to XMP (leading to GMP), while adenylosuccinate synthetase converts IMP to adenylosuccinate (leading to AMP).
- **Mutation Analysis:** The mutation involves a change from Guanine (G) to Adenine (A) at a specific position in the *IMPDH1* gene.
    - Guanine (G) and Adenine (A) are both purines.
    - Cytosine (C) and Thymine (T) (or Uracil (U) in RNA) are both pyrimidines.
- **Definition of Point Mutations:**
    - **Transition:** A point mutation where a purine base is substituted for another purine base (A ↔ G) or a pyrimidine base is substituted for another pyrimidine base (C ↔ T).
    - **Transversion:** A point mutation where a purine base is substituted for a pyrimidine base, or vice versa (A/G ↔ C/T).
    - **Frameshift mutation:** Insertion or deletion of nucleotides (not a multiple of 3) that shifts the reading frame during translation.
    - **Nonsense mutation:** A point mutation that changes a codon specifying an amino acid into a stop codon (UAA, UAG, UGA).
    - **Silent mutation:** A point mutation that changes a codon but does not change the encoded amino acid, often due to the degeneracy of the genetic code.
- **Applying Definitions:** The mutation G → A involves the substitution of one purine (G) for another purine (A). This fits the definition of a transition.
- **Pathophysiology:** Defective IMPDH leads to reduced GMP synthesis. This impairs the production of guanine nucleotides needed for DNA/RNA synthesis and energy (GTP). Muscle cells, with high energy demands, are particularly affected, leading to impaired ATP production, muscle damage (elevated CK, myoglobinuria), weakness, and fatigue. The autosomal recessive inheritance pattern is consistent with the father's similar condition and the patient's homozygous state.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Transversion:** Incorrect. A transversion involves the substitution of a purine for a pyrimidine or vice versa (e.g., G → C, A → T, C → G, T → A). The mutation G → A is a purine-to-purine substitution. Students might confuse the definitions if they don't carefully recall the base classifications (purine vs. pyrimidine).
- (C) **Frameshift mutation:** Incorrect. A frameshift mutation involves the insertion or deletion of one or two nucleotides (not a multiple of three), which alters the reading frame of the codons downstream of the mutation. The vignette describes a single nucleotide *substitution*, not an insertion or deletion. Students might confuse different types of mutations.
- (D) **Nonsense mutation:** Incorrect. A nonsense mutation changes an amino acid codon into a stop codon. The vignette describes a mutation in the *IMPDH1* gene, which encodes an enzyme (a protein). While the specific amino acid change caused by the G→A mutation at position 342 isn't explicitly stated, the question asks for the *type* of nucleotide change, not the consequence on the amino acid sequence or protein function. Even if the G→A change resulted in a stop codon, the primary classification of the *nucleotide* change itself is still important. The question asks about the *type* of point mutation (transition vs. transversion), not the effect on the protein sequence (nonsense, missense, silent). Students might focus on the protein consequence rather than the nucleotide change type.
- (E) **Silent mutation:** Incorrect. A silent mutation changes a codon but does not alter the amino acid sequence. While possible, the vignette describes a severe clinical phenotype (muscle disease) resulting from the mutation, suggesting it likely affects protein function (e.g., enzyme activity). A silent mutation is less likely to cause such a severe phenotype, although exceptions exist (e.g., affecting splicing or mRNA stability). More importantly, the question asks for the *type* of nucleotide substitution, and G→A is not necessarily silent; it depends on the specific codon and the resulting amino acid change. The primary classification based on the bases involved is transition. Students might incorrectly assume that any mutation causing a phenotype is non-silent or might not understand the distinction between the type of nucleotide change and its effect on the protein.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Remember the definitions of transition (purine ↔ purine or pyrimidine ↔ pyrimidine) and transversion (purine ↔ pyrimidine) point mutations. Identify the bases involved in the mutation described in the vignette.
- **Differentials:** Distinguish point mutations (substitutions) from insertions/deletions (frameshifts). Understand the consequences of different types of point mutations on the protein level (nonsense, missense, silent). Recognize that the *type* of nucleotide change (transition vs. transversion) is distinct from its effect on the amino acid sequence or protein function.

<a id='question-029'></a>

### Question 029: Silent (Synonymous) Mutation: Nucleotide Substitution Preserving Amino Acid Sequence Due to Degeneracy - Clinical Scenario 2
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the concept of synonymous (silent) mutations, their potential impact on mRNA splicing, stability, and translation rate, despite not altering the amino acid sequence itself. (理解同义突变的概念，以及它们对mRNA剪接、稳定性以及翻译速率的潜在影响，即使它们没有改变氨基酸序列。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old female presents to her primary care physician complaining of fatigue and mild jaundice. She has a history of recurrent episodes of hemolytic anemia since childhood, often triggered by oxidative stress (e.g., certain medications, infections). Family history is significant for similar symptoms in her maternal grandfather. Laboratory investigations reveal:
- Hemoglobin: 9.5 g/dL (Normal: 12-16 g/dL)
- Reticulocyte count: 5% (Normal: 0.5-2.5%)
- Peripheral blood smear: Heinz bodies, bite cells.
- Serum bilirubin: 2.0 mg/dL (Normal: 0.3-1.2 mg/dL)
- Liver function tests: Mildly elevated AST and ALT.
- Glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase (G6PD) activity assay: 25% of normal (Normal: 50-100%).
- Genetic testing reveals a heterozygous mutation in the *G6PD* gene. Sequencing of the patient's *G6PD* mRNA shows a single nucleotide substitution in the second codon (c.41G>A) that changes the codon from GGC to GGC. The resulting amino acid is Glycine (Gly).

Given these findings, which of the following mechanisms BEST explains the reduced G6PD enzyme activity in this patient despite the amino acid sequence remaining unchanged?

(A) Nonsense-mediated mRNA decay targeting the mRNA transcript due to the altered codon usage.
(B) Impaired mRNA splicing caused by the nucleotide substitution affecting a splice site.
(C) Reduced mRNA stability leading to decreased G6PD protein synthesis.
(D) Altered mRNA secondary structure affecting ribosome binding and translation efficiency.
(E) Increased G6PD protein degradation due to misfolding caused by the synonymous mutation.

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old female with fatigue, mild jaundice, recurrent hemolytic anemia triggered by oxidative stress, Heinz bodies, bite cells. This clinical picture strongly suggests Glucose-6-Phosphate Dehydrogenase (G6PD) deficiency.
- **Family History:** Maternal grandfather with similar symptoms suggests an X-linked recessive inheritance pattern, consistent with G6PD deficiency.
- **Lab Findings:** Low hemoglobin, high reticulocyte count (compensatory response to hemolysis), Heinz bodies (denatured hemoglobin precipitates), bite cells (splenic removal of Heinz bodies), elevated bilirubin (from heme breakdown), mildly elevated liver enzymes (secondary to hemolysis), and significantly reduced G6PD enzyme activity (25% of normal). These findings confirm G6PD deficiency and hemolytic anemia.
- **Genetic Findings:** Heterozygous mutation in the *G6PD* gene. Sequencing shows a c.41G>A substitution, changing the codon GGC to GGC. This means the codon codes for the same amino acid (Glycine). This is a synonymous or silent mutation.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism explaining *reduced* G6PD activity despite the *unchanged* amino acid sequence. This points to effects beyond the primary protein sequence.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **G6PD Deficiency:** G6PD is the rate-limiting enzyme in the pentose phosphate pathway, crucial for generating NADPH, which protects red blood cells from oxidative damage. Deficiency leads to hemolysis under oxidative stress.
- **Silent Mutations:** These are nucleotide substitutions that do not change the encoded amino acid due to the degeneracy of the genetic code (multiple codons can code for the same amino acid).
- **Impact of Silent Mutations:** While they don't alter the primary protein sequence, silent mutations can affect:
    - **mRNA splicing:** If the mutation occurs near a splice site, it can alter splicing efficiency or create new splice sites.
    - **mRNA stability:** Changes in codon usage can affect mRNA half-life.
    - **Translation rate:** Rare codons are translated more slowly. Synonymous mutations can change the codon usage pattern, affecting the speed of translation.
    - **mRNA secondary structure:** Codons can influence the folding of the mRNA molecule. Changes in secondary structure can affect ribosome binding, initiation of translation, or mRNA stability.
- **Specific Case:** The GGC to GGC change is a classic example where the synonymous mutation can affect translation. The GGC codon (Glycine) is relatively common. However, changes in codon context can alter the local mRNA secondary structure, potentially affecting ribosome binding or elongation speed. This can lead to reduced translation efficiency, resulting in lower levels of functional G6PD protein, even though the protein sequence itself is normal. This mechanism is well-documented for certain G6PD variants.
- **Why (D) is correct:** Altered mRNA secondary structure affecting ribosome binding and translation efficiency directly explains how a silent mutation can lead to reduced protein levels and enzyme activity without changing the amino acid sequence.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Nonsense-mediated mRNA decay (NMD)** is a surveillance pathway that degrades mRNAs containing premature stop codons. A synonymous mutation, by definition, does not create a stop codon. Therefore, NMD is not the mechanism here. This option confuses silent mutations with nonsense mutations.
- (B): **Impaired mRNA splicing** occurs when mutations affect splice sites (donor, acceptor, branch point) or regulatory elements. While silent mutations *can* affect splicing if located near splice sites, the vignette describes a mutation within a codon (c.41G>A), and there's no indication it affects splicing. The question specifically asks about the mechanism despite the *unchanged amino acid sequence*, which is the direct consequence of the mutation being synonymous and *not* affecting splicing.
- (C): **Reduced mRNA stability** can be caused by silent mutations altering mRNA secondary structure or affecting binding sites for RNA-binding proteins. While this *can* occur, it's often linked to changes in mRNA secondary structure (as in option D). Option (D) is more specific to the direct impact on the translation machinery (ribosome binding/efficiency), which is a common consequence of synonymous mutations affecting codon usage or context. Option (D) is a more direct and frequently cited mechanism for synonymous mutations impacting protein levels.
- (E): **Increased protein degradation** due to misfolding is typically associated with mutations that *do* change the amino acid sequence, leading to conformational instability. A silent mutation, by definition, does not alter the amino acid sequence, so it cannot directly cause misfolding leading to increased degradation. While some silent mutations might indirectly affect protein folding through effects on translation speed, the primary mechanism described in the vignette is reduced enzyme activity due to reduced protein synthesis, not increased degradation.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **Takeaway:** Silent mutations do not always result in silent phenotypes. They can affect mRNA processing, stability, or translation efficiency, leading to altered protein levels and function. (同义突变并不总是产生“沉默”的表型。它们会影响mRNA处理、稳定性或翻译效率，从而导致蛋白质水平和功能改变。)
- **Differentials:** Understand the difference between synonymous (silent), missense, and nonsense mutations. Recognize that silent mutations can have phenotypic consequences due to effects on mRNA splicing, stability, or translation kinetics (e.g., ribosome pausing, altered secondary structure). Contrast this with missense mutations (altered amino acid, potentially affecting protein structure/function) and nonsense mutations (premature stop codon, leading to truncated protein or NMD).
